review the docs/architecture.md doc against these writing guidelines: 1. Establish the "Logic of the Whole" (Minto Pyramid)
A design document is inherently a hierarchy of ideas. Use the Minto Pyramid Principle to ensure the reader can grasp your entire system architecture in the first 30 seconds
.
The Vertical Relationship: Every component description should answer a question raised by the level above it (e.g., if the top level describes a "Scalable Microservices Architecture," the next level must answer How it achieves that scale)
.
Structural Order: When dividing a system into parts, follow the MECE (Mutually Exclusive and Collectively Exhaustive) principle
. This ensures that your architectural layers (e.g., Frontend, Backend, Data) do not overlap and that no essential component is left out
.
Process Order: If your document describes a data flow or a sequence of operations, order the sections chronologically based on the system’s functioning
.
2. Narrative Flow via SCQ Logic
Avoid starting with a sterile list of data points
. Instead, use the Situation-Complication-Question (SCQ) framework for your introduction to justify the design's existence
.
The Problem Gap: Define the specific gap between the current technical state (R1) and the desired success state (R2)
. For example, instead of just stating a goal to "improve efficiency," provide a narrative: "Our API currently serves 1k RPS. Expected traffic is 10k RPS. To scale for this, we will re-architect..."
.
Internal Logic: Do not use "Complication" or "Question" as explicit headers; let the logic drive the narrative flow naturally
.
3. Sentence-Level Technical Precision
To make complex architectural concepts accessible, apply the principles of Clarity and Grace:
Characters as Subjects: In technical docs, "characters" are often the system, a user, or a specific service
. Make these characters the grammatical subjects of your sentences
. Avoid "dead" sentences where the action is hidden in nouns (nominalizations) like utilization or optimization
.
Old-before-New: Connect new technical concepts to what the reader already knows
. Start sentences with familiar context (the current state) and end them with the new, complex information (the proposed change)
.
The Stress Position: Place new technical terms or the most important concepts at the end of the sentence to give them emphasis
.
4. Balanced Concision vs. Integrity
While you should prune "verbal sludge" (e.g., changing "at this point in time" to "now"), you must never sacrifice operational integrity
.
Preserve Dependencies: Concision in a design doc must not lead to the removal of essential assumptions, technical dependencies, or risks
.
The "Hand-Product" Test: Every technical task or milestone mentioned in your document should imply a tangible end product (like a deployed service or a verified report) that the reader can visualize
.
5. Vocabulary and Tone
Avoid Cluttered Metaphors: Steer clear of overworked metaphors like "bottleneck," "blueprint," or "load-bearing," which can distract from technical reality
. Use Plain English alternatives like "shortage," "plan," or "essential"
.
Technical Humanity: Even a design document should sound like a professional talking to a peer, rather than a machine generating "volume-related production adjustments"
.
Sequential Logic: Science and technology writing is most effective when it leads the reader step-by-step through a sequence of deductions
. Start with the facts the reader must know before they can understand the next layer of the system
.

1	# Architecture
2	
3	Pre-read:[`docs/vision.md`](vision.md)).
4	
5	## 1. Two layers, one product
6	
7	The work splits into two layers:
8	
9	- **Layer 1 — supervision (`drain-cycle`).** Picks up a body of planned work - spawns a worker per phase per issue, holds the guardrails, halts/reverts/resumes/recovers, and records and grades the outcome. It is vendor-agnostic: each worker is a `claude -p` (or any equivalent) subprocess.
10	- **Layer 2 — workflow (the Shaper pack).** The intra-issue procedure, captured as composable skills: how a piece of work goes from picked-up to merged PR. This is the knowledge that used to live in the operator's head, written down once.
11	
12	The vision's three pieces map onto this split: "one place for state" is the state plane (§6), "the steps become skills" is Layer 2, and "the supervision becomes a supervisor" is Layer 1.
13	
14	## 2. The artifact boundary
15	
16	The two layers meet at an artifact, not at a function call. **Layer 2 writes signals; Layer 1 reads them.** The supervisor never reads the workflow's steps — only the artifacts the workflow leaves behind: Linear issue state, run-log fields, and `.drain-handoff.json` (e.g. `pr_urls`).
17	
18	The placement test for any new behaviour: does `drain-cycle` need to know *what a role does* (Layer 2), or only *whether an artifact exists* (Layer 1)? Submitting a PR is "what a role does" — it belongs in a skill. Reading back the submitted PR URLs to decide whether to advance is "whether an artifact exists" — it belongs in the supervisor (see design decision §19).
19	
20	Keeping the boundary at the artifact is what makes Layer 1 content-blind, and content-blindness is what lets the same Layer 2 run under any worker.
21	
22	## 3. Dual-mode: the same skills by hand or unattended
23	
24	Because the workflow is skills and the supervisor only reads artifacts, the same skills run two ways: the operator invokes them at the keyboard, or a spawned worker runs them unattended. There is one workflow, exercised two ways — not a manual path and a separate automated path that drift apart.
25	
26	Design decision §10 (config symlinked into each worktree) is what makes the headless worker's environment match an interactive session, so a skill behaves the same in both modes.
27	
28	## 4. Layer 2 — the workflow pack
29	
30	The pack carries two verb namespaces, one per half of the lifecycle:
31	
32	- **Planning — `shape:*`.** Four front-door skills: `shape:idea`, `shape:project`, `shape:design`, `shape:delivery`. (Consolidation tracked by the "Consolidate Shaper's Lifecycle into Four Phase Skills" project.)
33	
34	- **Execution — `exec:*`.** The intra-issue graph, named delegations only, no inlined procedure:
35	  
36	  ```
37	  exec:pickup → exec:breakdown → exec:build → (exec:debug | exec:simplify)
38	              → exec:review → exec:verify → exec:finish
39	  ```
40	  
41	  `exec:pickup` is the front door; `exec:review` fans out reviewer personas; `exec:finish` is Graphite-first with a plain-git fallback and produces the trail artefacts (What/Why/Focus PR body, review-summary comment, Linear status move). The execution namespace is pinned by ADR 0004 / the Shaper `execution-workflow` design doc; that doc is authoritative for the graph and the handoff contract.
42	
43	Every step is also a standalone skill, so a human can run any one by hand. A **handoff envelope** carries the issue's acceptance criteria from pickup through to review, so the spec-compliance persona can grade *built-the-wrong-thing*, not just *built-it-badly*.
44	
45	## 5. Layer 1 — the supervisor
46	
47	`drain-cycle` owns process concerns only: spawn, guardrails, halt, revert, resume, recover, record, grade. It reads Linear state and the worker's artifacts to decide whether to advance to the next issue or stop. It does not contain workflow prose — the worker's prompt points at the entry skill and the procedure lives in Layer 2.
48	
49	The supervisor mechanics are the bulk of `docs/design-decisions.md`: worktree-per-issue (§3), resource guardrails (§9), group-kill on breach (§8), resume-on-rerun (§14), blocks-aware ordering (§12), and the watch overlay (§15).
50	
51	## 6. The state plane
52	
53	Everything works off one organized record of where the work stands. **Linear is authoritative** for issue status (design decision §1, §12). Beneath it sit the durable supervisor records: per-run logs (§4, §8), the atomic active-run marker (§11), and opt-in OpenTelemetry traces (§13).
54	
55	These records are the **delayed feedback loop** that replaces live watching. Automating supervision deliberately gives up the operator's real-time view of each worker; observability is how that trade is paid back. Better observability widens the set of work that is safe to hand off (§8).
56	
57	## 7. The keystone
58	
59	The move that realizes the whole split: **`prompt.py` becomes a thin pointer at `exec:pickup`** instead of inlining the workflow. The supervisor stops carrying procedure; the pack owns it; any vendor's worker follows the same prose. This is tracked by the "drain-cycle supervises; the pack owns the workflow" project and is the run-first step — everything else in this doc is a facet of it. Until it lands, the supervisor's prompt still names a few skills directly (e.g. the current `/code-review-and-quality` and `/shape:pr-finishing`); those references swap to `exec:*` at the keystone cutover.
60	
61	## 8. The supervisor's autonomy horizon
62	
63	How far the supervisor runs without the operator. Today it drains a cycle transactionally — one issue, one worker, one diff — and halts when it runs out of runnable work or hits a result it cannot get past. The horizon extends outward as observability earns more trust: building on unmerged work, auto-merging trusted classes, and eventually a resident process that watches its own PRs through the review-and-merge loop. That extension is shaped in [`ideas/drain-past-the-merge-gate.md`](ideas/drain-past-the-merge-gate.md).
64	
65	The horizon is a deliberate trade, not a default: autonomy is applied selectively where the delayed feedback loop (§6) makes the loss of live intervention acceptable.
66	
67	## 9. Phase separation: a worker per phase, not a worker per issue
68	
69	A worker does not run the whole `exec:*` chain. Each phase — code, review, finish — is its own spawn, with its own model tier, and the agent that produced an artifact never judges it. Two independent reasons force this:
70	
71	- **Independent verification.** A coder reviewing its own work rationalises its own choices. Spawning review as a separate agent that did not write the code makes review adversarial by construction, not by prompt wording.
72	- **Per-phase model economics.** Review anchors to a stronger (more expensive) model regardless of what coded the change. That asymmetry is only expressible if each phase is its own spawn with its own `--model` pin — a cheap model can build while an expensive one reviews.
73	
74	The three phase agents map onto the existing pack: code = `exec:build` (+`exec:debug`, `exec:simplify`), review = `exec:review`, finish = `exec:finish`. Each is a goal-shaped worker prompt, not a resident process — the goal ("produce sliced artifacts", "apply every quality lens", "land human-readable PRs") drives a sequence of skill delegations inside one spawn.
75	
76	The cost accepted in return: every phase boundary pays a spawn plus artifact rehydration, so the handoff envelope (§2) must carry everything the next phase needs — a single worker kept that context in memory for free. This is the deliberate price of independence; see design decision §20.
77	
78	## 10. The resident control plane
79	
80	The supervisor is moving from a one-shot CLI to a resident process — the autonomy horizon of §8 made concrete. Two scopes, both Layer 1:
81	
82	- **Control plane (one per machine).** The long-lived daemon. Owns process lifecycle, the queue of planned units to execute, and an API the operator queries and steers: what is running, halt this issue, resume, and (the horizon behaviour) watch open PRs through the review-and-merge loop and respond to review comments.
83	- **Execution-coordinator (one per unit in flight).** Spawned by the control plane to drive a single cycle or project. It advances work by reading artifacts (§2) — never by reading inside a phase — and halts on a missing or failed artifact.
84	
85	The control plane stays a **process, not a Claude skill** (design decision §22). A `/execute-cycle` skill would run inside a Claude session and collapse the artifact boundary that makes the worker vendor-agnostic; the one-command ergonomics come instead from a thin CLI front-door (`drain-cycle run <unit>`). "Respond to PR comments" is a control-plane behaviour, not a pack skill, because it requires watching a PR after it is open — which only a resident process does.
86	
87	## 11. Multi-altitude review: the dual of the delivery hierarchy
88	
89	`shape:delivery` decomposes committed work *downward* — project → milestones → nodes → tasks. Verification rolls *upward* along the same tree, with the review altitude matching the decomposition altitude:
90	
91	| Altitude             | Fires when                                   | Lenses                                                                                                                             |
92	| -------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
93	| **Task** (per issue) | a task's diff is ready, before its PR merges | spec-compliance · security · reliability/resilience · code-quality · outcome — diff-bounded                                        |
94	| **Milestone**        | a milestone's last child task lands          | integration/coherence/acceptance (the task lens raised a level) · regression (did landing degrade anything outside this boundary?) |
95	| **Project**          | a project's last milestone lands             | architecture review · measurable stated goals (partial — some goals take time and are deferred)                                    |
96	
97	Work fans down; verification rolls up. The trigger is structural — a parent's review fires exactly when its last child completes — and the hierarchy itself is authoritative in Linear (milestones, projects), so the state plane (§6) reads it rather than holding a second copy.
98	
99	Three consequences distinguish higher-altitude review from task review:
100	
101	1. **The execution-coordinator is a tree walker, not a queue drainer.** It models the hierarchy, detects "this milestone's last child landed", and fires the milestone review.
102	2. **Higher-altitude review produces new work, not reverts.** A task review can halt before a PR merges. A milestone or project review runs *after* its child PRs have merged — a merged slice cannot be cleanly reverted — so a failing review emits new remediation issues slotted back into the plan. This is a different halt semantic from task-level, and it widens the open seam below.
103	3. **Project review is partial and deferred.** Some goals cannot be measured at completion. Project review measures what it can now and *schedules* the rest, which is only possible with the resident control plane (§10).
104	
105	Two unknowns here are not yet decided and are shaped as spikes, not committed: the **blast-radius definition** for regression review (it is not diff-bounded like task review), and **remediation routing** (what gets created on a failing altitude review, and whether the parent pauses or its siblings keep draining). See design decision §21.
106	
107	## 12. The execution unit: a cycle or a project
108	
109	The supervisor executes a *planned unit*. The atom is unchanged — one issue, with a worker per phase (§9) — and a cycle and a project differ only as containers with a hierarchy over them. "Drain a cycle" is one entry point, not the definition; project execution is out of scope today but is a later container, not a redesign (design decision §22). The tool keeps the name `drain-cycle`; the concept it serves is wider.
110	
111	## Known open seam
112	
113	The one undecided boundary crossing: how Layer-2 verdicts (`outcome_verdict`, `prep_verdict`) travel from a skill into the run-log. Today `handoff.py` carries only `pr_urls`. The verdict-handoff schema is a one-way door for the correctness work (the "Multi-agent collaboration for correctness" Layer-1 project) and should be decided before that work starts.
114	
115	The multi-altitude reviews (§11) widen this same seam: a milestone or project verdict has to cross the boundary too, and a failing one routes *remediation work* back into the plan rather than recording a pass/fail on a single diff. Whatever schema resolves the task-level verdict handoff should be designed to carry the higher-altitude verdicts as well.
116	
```

I read the whole doc. Here's a review against your five guideline areas, ordered by impact. Line references are clickable.

## 1. Logic of the whole (Minto) — the biggest gap

**No 30-second governing thesis.** The doc opens straight into `## 1. Two layers, one product` with no top-line sentence that states the whole architecture before decomposing it. Minto wants the answer first, then the support. `architecture.md:5` is your real thesis ("two layers, one product, split on an artifact boundary") — but it's buried as a section title, not stated as the document's lead. A reader can't grasp the system in 30 seconds because there's no single summarizing sentence to grasp.

**Recommendation:** add a 2-3 sentence lead before §1: the thesis (a supervision layer over a workflow layer, meeting at an artifact), why that split exists, and the keystone move that realizes it. That lead is the apex of the pyramid; every section below already answers a question it would raise.

**Ordering isn't quite MECE / vertical.** The current order interleaves *what the layers are* with *how they meet* and *where it's going*:

- §1 layers → §2 boundary → §3 dual-mode → §4 Layer 2 → §5 Layer 1 → ...

§4 and §5 *define* the two layers but arrive two sections after §2 already reasons about the boundary between them, and after §3 depends on both. The reader meets "Layer 2 writes signals; Layer 1 reads them" (`architecture.md:16`) before either layer is described. A more vertical order: **define both layers (1, 4, 5) → how they meet (2, 3, 7) → how it evolves (8, 9, 10, 11, 12)**. Right now §9 (phase separation) and §10 (control plane) are both Layer-1 elaborations sitting far from §5.

## 2. SCQ narrative — missing entirely

There is no Situation–Complication framing. The doc offloads all of it to the `Pre-read` link and starts with a flat assertion. Per your own SCQ rule, the design's *existence* should be justified by the gap it closes. One or two sentences would do it — the current state ("the operator's head holds the workflow; supervision is manual and live") versus the desired state ("the workflow is written down once and a supervisor runs it unattended"). You don't need explicit "Complication"/"Question" headers — just let the gap drive the opening. Note §1's last sentence (`architecture.md:12`) and the vision already contain this; it's a matter of surfacing one line at the top.

## 3. Sentence-level precision — mostly strong, a few fixes

The doc is genuinely good here: system-as-subject, active voice, tight. Specific catches:

- `architecture.md:9` — "Picks up a body of planned work **-** spawns a worker" uses a stray hyphen as a clause separator. Use a comma or em dash.
- `architecture.md:32` — "(**Consolidation** tracked by the ... project.)" is a dead nominalization. Reverse to a verb: "The ... project tracks this." Same pattern reads better throughout.
- `architecture.md:9` — "holds the guardrails, halts/reverts/resumes/recovers, and records and grades the outcome" packs five slashed verbs into one clause. It's a label-dump, not a sentence. Consider splitting the responsibilities into two short sentences (process control vs. record/grade).

Old-before-new and stress position are handled well — e.g. §7's "**`prompt.py` becomes a thin pointer at `exec:pickup`**" lands the new term in the stress position.

## 4. Concision vs. integrity — fine

No amputated dependencies. Risks and unknowns are explicitly preserved — the "Known open seam" section and the §11 "two unknowns ... shaped as spikes" are exactly the integrity the guideline asks for. No verbal sludge worth cutting.

## 5. Vocabulary and tone — metaphor density is the watch item

The doc leans hard on spatial/structural metaphors: **keystone** (§7), **horizon** (§8), **seam / open seam** (§11), **altitude** (§11), **plane** (state plane, control plane), **tree walker**, **front door**, **guardrails**. Your guideline explicitly steers away from overworked metaphors like "load-bearing" (which also appears in your global WRITING.md watchlist).

Balanced call: some are load-bearing domain terms doing real work and consistently — **altitude** maps cleanly onto the delivery hierarchy and earns its place; **plane** is established. But two are decorative where plain English is clearer:
- **keystone** (§7) → "the move that realizes the split" already says it; the masonry image adds nothing.
- **open seam** (§11, "Known open seam") → "the one undecided boundary crossing" (which you already write in the body) is plainer than the metaphor in the heading.

Also: your headings are labels, not achievements (your own rule: "Headings are achievements, not labels"). "§6 The state plane", "§7 The keystone" name a category. Compare with the achievement style: "§9 Phase separation: a worker per phase, not a worker per issue" — that one carries the claim. The strongest headings here already do this; the noun-only ones (§2, §6, §7, §10) could.

## One non-style bug

`architecture.md:3` — the Pre-read link is malformed: `Pre-read:[`docs/vision.md`](vision.md)).` Missing space after the colon, backticks inside the link text, and a stray trailing `)`. Renders broken.

---

**Net:** sentence-level craft is already strong; the gap is structural — no apex thesis and no SCQ opening, so the reader assembles the whole from parts instead of being handed it first. Fixing the lead + reordering into define→meet→evolve would do the most.

## Reconciliation Report: Architecture Diagram vs. Code/Docs Terminology

### 1. **Resident Daemon / Process Manager**

**Diagram says:** "process manager"  
**Code/Docs actually say:**
- **architecture.md §10**: "**Control plane** (one per machine). The long-lived daemon."
- **design-decisions.md §22**: "A process with a CLI front-door, not a `/execute-cycle` skill" — the front-door is `drain-cycle run <unit>`
- **No use of "process manager"** anywhere in the codebase

**Status:** Diagram terminology is non-standard; code/docs use **"control plane"** exclusively.

---

### 2. **Per-Unit Coordinator**

**Diagram says:** "execution-coordinator"  
**Code/Docs actually say:**
- **architecture.md §10**: "**Execution-coordinator** (one per unit in flight). Spawned by the control plane to drive a single cycle or project."
- **architecture.md §11**: "The execution-coordinator is a tree walker, not a queue drainer."
- Code uses this term in `orchestrator.py` conceptually but not as an explicit class/object name

**Status:** Matches precisely. ✓

---

### 3. **CLI Front Door**

**Diagram says:** implicit (not labeled)  
**Code/Docs actually say:**
- **architecture.md §10**: "`drain-cycle run <unit>`" is the thin CLI front-door
- **No separate skill** — the supervisor is a process, not a Claude skill
- Current `prompt.py` still inlines workflow (pre-keystone); targets `exec:pickup` at keystone cutover

**Status:** Diagram is silent; docs/code define it as `drain-cycle run <unit>`.

---

### 4. **Exec:* Skill Chain**

**Diagram shows phases:** "exec:build", "exec:review", "exec:finish"  
**Code/Docs actually define the full chain** (architecture.md §4, line 34–39):

```
exec:pickup → exec:breakdown → exec:build → (exec:debug | exec:simplify)
            → exec:review → exec:verify → exec:finish
```

**Breakdown:**
- **Code phase:** `exec:build` + `exec:debug`/`exec:simplify` (per architecture.md §9)
- **Review phase:** `exec:review` (fans out to reviewer personas; architecture.md §4)
- **Finish phase:** `exec:finish` (Graphite-first, produces trail artifacts; architecture.md §4)

**Status:** Diagram captures only the three phase-level skills; the full chain includes `exec:pickup`, `exec:breakdown`, `exec:verify` as intermediate steps before/after phases.

---

### 5. **Phase Names**

**Diagram says:** "exec:build", "exec:review", "exec:finish"  
**Code/Docs actually say:**
- **architecture.md §9**: "Each phase — **code, review, finish** — is its own spawn"
- The phase *names* in prose are **"code", "review", "finish"**
- The *skill entry points* per phase are **`exec:build`, `exec:review`, `exec:finish`**

**Status:** Diagram uses skill names correctly; architecture.md clarifies the underlying phase names are "code", "review", "finish".

## Summary

I've explored the drain-cycle documentation context. Here are my findings:

---

### 1. **docs/vision.md** — Structure and Lead

**Structure (headings):**
- # Vision
- ## Problem
- ## Solution

**Opening lead paragraph:**
> "Doing a piece of work is never a single action. It's a sequence of steps - prompt an agent to write the code, review it against the right standards, open a PR, rewrite the PR description so a person can read it, and on from there. My agent handles any one of these steps well, but only one at a time, and only after I've set it up. Each step is its own conversation: I prompt it, refine what I mean, check what comes back."

**Problem framing (Situation/Complication):**
The Problem section frames three interdependent issues the operator currently owns:
1. **Holding state** (what's done, what's next, what's blocked)
2. **Knowing the steps** (what they are, order, standards)
3. **Supervising the run** (moving work between steps)

This splits into two consequences: **execution inconsistency** (care depends on the operator's daily discipline) and **operator pinned to supervision** (can't step back to think about next projects).

The Solution section maps these three problems to three architectures: state plane, skills, and supervisor — which your architecture.md then elaborates on.

**What your architecture.md can lean on:** The entire vision frame is established. You can reference the three-piece decomposition, the Situation (manual supervision holding operator), and the dual-mode (before/after diagrams showing the operator's position in the workflow). You need NOT restate the problem.

---

### 2. **docs/design-decisions.md** — Numbered Decisions (All 22)

The section-number scheme is simply `## <number>. <Title>`. All 22 exist:

| § | Title |
|---|-------|
| 1 | The spawned agent updates Linear itself |
| 2 | `--dangerously-skip-permissions` is accepted |
| 3 | Fresh worktree per issue, not a shared workspace |
| 4 | Run-log is one file per invocation, not one file per cycle |
| 5 | Each issue declares its target repo via a `repo:<name>` Linear label |
| 6 | Installed as a `uv tool`, with the secret read from `~/.drain-cycle/.env` |
| 7 | Workers default to Sonnet; a `model:` label overrides per issue |
| 8 | Workers use stream-json output; usage is parsed from the wire and the worker leads its own process group |
| 9 | Resource guardrails: a native cost belt and orchestrator token/time suspenders |
| 10 | Headless workers inherit project-scoped config by symlink |
| 11 | Active-run marker lives above `runs/` as `~/.drain-cycle/active.json` |
| 12 | Execution order: manual drag-order only, blocks-aware |
| 13 | Opt-in OpenTelemetry tracing to Honeycomb |
| 14 | Halted issues resume on re-run by reusing the preserved worktree |
| 15 | `--watch` runs claude *in* the tmux pane, not a formatter tailing a log |
| 16 | The Graphite PR-stacking sequence the orchestrator will run (ABA-300 spike) |
| 17 | `/shape:task` runs inside the worker session; whole-project shaping stays at design-doc scope |
| 18 | PR links are recorded in the run-log and posted to Linear by the orchestrator |
| 19 | The worker owns PR submission via the finishing skill; the orchestrator reads `pr_urls` back |
| 20 | A worker per phase, not a worker per issue |
| 21 | Review is multi-altitude; higher-altitude review yields new work, not reverts |
| 22 | The supervisor stays a process executing a planned unit; it is not a Claude skill |

All cross-references in architecture.md should verify against these exact titles.

---

# Audit: SCQ-A completeness of the architecture-doc rewrite plan

The plan's only SCQ-relevant payload is section **1a** (the lead) plus the **Context** framing in lines 5–12. I audit that intended lead against each of S, C, Q, A, then list gaps and conflicts. The reusable raw material lives in `vision.md` lines 5–20 (Problem) and 22–30 (Solution).

## A. Per-element evaluation

### S — Situation (the stable starting point)

**What the plan delivers:** Implicitly, via the C-clause. The plan's SCQ bullet (line 31) is: *"today the operator holds the workflow in their head and supervises every run live."* That is the only "before" state in the plan, and it is folded into the complication rather than established first as neutral, accepted context.

**Verdict: weak / conflated.** Minto's Situation must be a non-controversial fact the reader already accepts, stated *before* the tension. "The operator holds the workflow in their head and supervises every run live" is already the problem — it is R1, not the stable ground. There is no genuine "this is how it works and it's fine" opening.

**What's reusable from vision.md (the true Situation):** vision.md line 5 gives the textbook stable starting point —
> "Doing a piece of work is never a single action. It's a sequence of steps - prompt an agent to write the code, review it against the right standards, open a PR, rewrite the PR description so a person can read it... My agent handles any one of these steps well, but only one at a time, and only after I've set it up."

And line 7: *"This holds up fine for small work."*

**Proposed Situation sentence(s):** "Shipping a piece of agentic work is a sequence of steps — code it, review it against the right standards, open the PR, make the description readable — and a capable agent handles any one step well, one at a time. For small work, an operator driving each step by hand holds up fine." That is the shared, accepted ground the reader nods at before the disturbance.

### C — Complication (the disturbing event / R1)

**What the plan delivers (line 31):** *"today the operator holds the workflow in their head and supervises every run live."* The plan labels this "the gap, leaned on vision.md not restated."

**Verdict: partial.** It names a steady-state burden but not a *change* or *disturbing event*. Minto's Complication is the thing that breaks the stable state. vision.md supplies exactly that trigger and it is missing from the plan: vision.md line 7 — *"the more complex the work gets... the faster that workflow breaks down. And when it breaks, it's clear the thing holding the work together was me all along, doing three jobs I'd never thought to separate."*

The plan also omits the *two concrete undesired results* vision.md states explicitly (lines 17–18), which are the sharpest R1 statement available:
> 1. "Execution is inconsistent... the same kind of work gets a different level of care depending on the day."
> 2. "I'm pinned to supervising... I can't do much else while my workers run."

**Proposed Complication:** "But as work grows more complex, that breaks down — and what breaks reveals that the operator was personally holding three fused jobs together: the state, the steps, and the supervision. Two costs follow: execution quality depends on the operator's discipline that day, and the operator stays pinned to driving instead of planning the next work." This is a real disturbing event (complexity rising) producing a named R1, not just a description of routine.

### Q — Question (the pivot)

**What the plan delivers:** **Nothing.** The plan never names a question. Line 8 of Context says "No SCQ opening... the problem... is offloaded entirely to the Pre-read link," and 1a promises "SCQ (one or two sentences, no explicit headers)" — but no candidate question text appears anywhere in the plan. The audit prompt's suspicion is correct: **the pivot is implied but never named.**

This is the single largest SCQ gap. Without naming the question (even as internal logic), the writer has no test for whether the Answer actually closes it, and "remind not inform" cannot be checked against a target.

**Proposed Question (the implicit pivot this doc answers):** *"How do you build the connector so the workflow runs the same way every time without a person driving it — and how is that system structured?"* vision.md line 24 frames it natively: *"The way out is to stop being the thing that holds the work together and build that thing once instead."* The architecture doc is the "how is it structured" answer to that, so the question should pivot from vision's *whether/why* to architecture's *how it's built*. Recommend the plan add an explicit instruction: "1a must contain (as narrative, not a header) the question the rest of the doc answers — name it."

### A — Answer (R2, the main point)

**What the plan delivers (lines 30–31):** Thesis = *"a supervision layer (Layer 1, drain-cycle) over a workflow layer (Layer 2, the Shaper pack), meeting at an artifact boundary; the keystone move (prompt.py becomes a thin pointer at exec:pickup) realizes the split."* And R2 = *"the architecture writes the workflow down once and lets a content-blind supervisor run it unattended."*

**Verdict: structurally good thesis, but the R2 outcome is vague — confirmed.** The structural apex (two layers + artifact boundary + keystone) is specific and is genuinely the doc's main point. But "a content-blind supervisor runs it unattended" describes a *mechanism*, not a *measured result*. Minto wants R2 stated specifically and where possible quantifiably — closing the R1→R2 gap. The two R1 costs from vision.md have natural quantified mirrors that the plan ignores:

- R1: "execution quality depends on the day" → **R2:** "every piece of work gets the same level of care on every run" (vision.md line 69: *"the work gets more consistent than I am"*).
- R1: "operator pinned to supervising" → **R2:** "the operator is needed once at the start and again only when a run halts on something it can't get past" (vision.md lines 60–63: *"I'm there once at the start, and again only when something genuinely needs me"*).

**Proposed sharper R2:** "The result: the workflow is captured once and a content-blind supervisor drives it unattended — so execution is consistent on every run instead of varying with operator discipline, and the operator's attention is needed only to point the system at planned work and to step in when a run halts." That explicitly closes both R1 gaps and is the kind of specific outcome Minto asks for. Recommend the plan instruct 1a to bind R2 to the two vision.md undesired results by name.

### The "remind, not inform" rule vs. the diagram — this is a real conflict

**The plan (line 32, line 76):** *"Immediately follow the lead with the overview image (Diagram 1) so prose-thesis and picture land together"* / "Placed right after the apex lead." Diagram 1 is described (lines 75–86) as a dense structure: control plane → execution-coordinator → nested Project ⊃ Milestone ⊃ Task with three phase spawns, plus five task review lenses, three milestone lenses, two project lenses, and PR-comment routing.

**The conflict is genuine.** Minto's intro must *remind* — no new data/structure the reader must independently absorb to accept the premise. Diagram 1 is the opposite: it is the most information-dense new exhibit in the entire doc. Treating it as part of the intro means the reader must decode a full system map within the "first 30 seconds," which defeats the 30-second-grasp goal rather than serving it. The plan's own line 10 reasoning ("a corrected overview diagram is exactly the Minto apex") conflates *apex* (the one-sentence governing thesis, which belongs in the intro) with *the full decomposition* (which is body).

**Recommended resolution (matches the audit prompt's suggestion):** The intro lead is **pure prose SCQ-A** and must stand alone — a reader who never sees the image still gets context + problem + the structural thesis in 30 seconds. Diagram 1 then becomes **the first body element**, immediately after the intro, as the visual expansion of the apex — not a component of the intro. The prose thesis (line 30: two layers + artifact boundary + keystone) is the legitimate Minto apex; the diagram supports it. Recommend the plan explicitly demote the diagram from "part of the lead" to "first body element after the lead," and add an acceptance check: *the lead reads completely and passes the 30-second test with images stripped.*

## B. Gaps the plan should incorporate but doesn't

1. **Name the pivot Question.** 1a promises SCQ but supplies no question. Add explicit instruction + candidate text (see A-Q). Without it there is no target the Answer is measured against.

2. **Establish a true Situation before the complication.** The plan jumps straight to the burdened state. Add the "sequence of steps... holds up fine for small work" opener from vision.md lines 5–7.

3. **Make the Complication a change, not a steady state.** Pull in vision.md line 7's "the more complex the work gets, the faster that workflow breaks down" as the disturbing event, and the two named undesired results (vision.md lines 17–18).

4. **Quantify/specify R2 against R1.** Bind the answer to the two costs it removes (consistency; attention freed), per vision.md lines 69–73. The plan's current R2 names a mechanism, not an outcome.

5. **Add an explicit 30-second / standalone-lead acceptance test for 1a.** Verification step 4 (line 99) asks "does the lead give the whole in ~30s" but only as a vague read-through, and it currently assumes the diagram is part of that grasp. Reframe it to test the *prose alone*.

6. **Bridge vision's three-piece frame into the apex.** architecture.md line 12 already maps vision's three pieces (state plane / Layer 2 / Layer 1) onto the split. The plan's two-layer thesis silently drops "state" as a co-equal third piece, yet §6 (the state plane) and vision.md line 26 ("This is the ground the rest stands on") treat state as foundational. The lead should decide whether state is part of the apex or subordinate — right now it's ambiguous, and a reader who knows vision.md will notice the third piece vanished.

## C. Conflicts with SCQ-A best practices to correct

1. **Diagram-in-intro vs. remind-not-inform** (detailed in A). The plan places the densest new exhibit inside the 30-second window. Move it to first body element; keep the intro pure prose.

2. **Situation conflated with Complication** (A-S). The plan's single "today the operator..." sentence is asked to be both the stable ground and the problem. Minto requires them sequenced and distinct; split them.

3. **"Leaned on vision.md not restated" risks under-reminding** (line 31). The plan's instinct to not restate vision.md is right in spirit (avoid duplicating the "why" doc) but it currently leans so hard that the architecture intro would carry *no* self-contained problem statement — re-offloading to the Pre-read link, which is the exact defect Context line 8 says the rewrite exists to fix. The intro must *remind* the reader of the gap in one or two self-contained sentences, even though the full argument lives in vision.md. "Lean on, don't restate" should become "compress to a one-line reminder that stands alone."

The diagram placement is the one item that actively contradicts a best practice and should be corrected by demoting the diagram out of the intro.

## Approved Plan (edited by user):
# Plan: restructure `docs/architecture.md` for the writing guidelines + simple visuals

## Context

`docs/architecture.md` is the "how" companion to `docs/vision.md` (the "why") and is cross-tied to all 22 entries in `docs/design-decisions.md`. A review against five writing guidelines (Minto pyramid, SCQ narrative, sentence-level precision, concision-vs-integrity, vocabulary/tone) found the sentence-level craft already strong but two **structural** gaps:

1. **No apex thesis / no 30-second grasp.** The doc opens straight into `## 1. Two layers, one product` with no governing sentence and no overview. A reader assembles the whole from parts instead of being handed it first.
2. **No SCQ opening.** The problem the architecture exists to solve is offloaded entirely to the `Pre-read` link.

Separately, the owner wants the doc to be **complemented by simple committed visuals** (hand-drawn / Excalidraw aesthetic, like the supplied sketch). The visual is not decoration: the *prose* lead is the Minto apex (the one-paragraph governing thesis), and a corrected overview diagram is its visual expansion — placed as the first body element after the lead, not inside the intro (Minto's "remind, not inform" rule; see §1a).

The supplied sketch diverges from the authoritative doc and must be redrawn to match it (see "Diagram corrections" — the doc wins). Outcome: an architecture doc that leads with its thesis, flows define → meet → evolve, reads in plain professional prose, and is anchored by one accurate overview image plus one or two supporting diagrams.

## Decisions locked with the owner

- **Visual format:** committed images (hand-drawn/Excalidraw look) under a new `docs/images/` (or `docs/diagrams/`) dir, referenced from the markdown. Commit an editable source next to each rendered export so the image stays maintainable.
- **Terminology:** the doc is authoritative. Redraw the sketch to match doc vocabulary exactly; do **not** introduce the sketch's friendly/invented labels.
- **Rewrite depth:** full restructure (reorder sections, add apex + SCQ lead, achievement headings), not a surgical patch.

## Files

- `docs/architecture.md` — the rewrite (only prose file changed).
- `docs/images/` (new) — committed diagram exports + editable sources.
- No code changes. `docs/vision.md` and `docs/design-decisions.md` are read-only references; all 22 `§` cross-refs in architecture.md were verified accurate and must stay accurate after the reorder.

## Part 1 — Prose restructure (`docs/architecture.md`)

### 1a. Add the apex + SCQ-A lead (new, before `## 1`)

The lead is **pure prose** and must stand alone — a reader who never sees a diagram still gets context + problem + structural answer in the first 30 seconds. Write it as a full Situation→Complication→Question→Answer runway, **no explicit S/C/Q/A headers** (internal logic only). The job is to *remind* the reader of the gap in one or two self-contained sentences, not to re-offload it to the `Pre-read` link (the exact defect this rewrite fixes) and not to duplicate vision.md's full argument. Source material below is from `docs/vision.md`; compress, don't paste.

- **Situation (stable ground first, non-controversial).** Shipping a piece of agentic work is a *sequence* of steps — code it, review it against the right standards, open the PR, make the description readable — and a capable agent handles any one step well, one at a time. Driving each step by hand holds up fine for small work. *(vision.md:5–7.)*
- **Complication (a change, not a steady state).** As the work grows more complex the hand-driven workflow breaks down — and what breaks reveals the operator was personally holding three fused jobs together: the **state**, the **steps**, and the **supervision**. Two undesired results follow (R1): execution quality varies with the operator's discipline that day, and the operator stays pinned to driving instead of planning the next work. *(vision.md:7, 17–18.)*
- **Question (name the pivot explicitly — do not leave it implied).** The doc must contain, as narrative, the question the rest of it answers: *how do you build the thing that holds the work together once — so the workflow runs the same way every time without a person driving it — and how is that system structured?* *(pivots from vision.md:24 "stop being the thing that holds the work together and build that thing once instead".)*
- **Answer (apex thesis + a specific R2, closing the R1 gap).** Structural apex: a supervision layer (Layer 1, `drain-cycle`) over a workflow layer (Layer 2, the Shaper pack), meeting at an artifact boundary, with the **state plane** as the shared record both work off (keep state as the co-equal third piece vision.md:12/§6 names — do not silently drop it); the keystone move (`prompt.py` becomes a thin pointer at `exec:pickup`) realizes the split. R2 must be an *outcome*, not just a mechanism, and bound to the two R1 costs: the workflow is captured once and a content-blind supervisor drives it unattended, so **execution is consistent on every run** instead of varying with operator discipline, and the operator is **needed only to point the system at planned work and to step in when a run halts**. *(vision.md:60–63, 69–73.)*

A corrected redraw of the supplied sketch. `user → control plane → execution-coordinator → nested Project ⊃ Milestone ⊃ Task`, Task showing the three phase spawns, plus the higher review altitudes. Placed right after the apex lead.

1	# Vision
2	
3	## Problem
4	
5	Doing a piece of work is never a single action. It's a sequence of steps - prompt an agent to write the code, review it against the right standards, open a PR, rewrite the PR description so a person can read it, and on from there. My agent handles any one of these steps well, but only one at a time, and only after I've set it up. Each step is its own conversation: I prompt it, refine what I mean, check what comes back.
6	
7	This holds up fine for small work. But the more complex the work gets - the more thinking and planning it needs up front - the faster that workflow breaks down. And when it breaks, it's clear the thing holding the work together was me all along, doing three jobs I'd never thought to separate:
8	
9	1. **Holding the state** - where the work stands: what's done, what's next, what's blocked. Ideally this lives in one organized place. By default, in agentic development, it scatters into loose markdown files that are painful to keep straight.
10	2. **Knowing the steps** - what the steps are, what order they run in, and the standard each one has to meet. That knowledge lives in my head and comes out a little differently every time.
11	3. **Supervising the run** - moving each piece of work from one step to the next, pushing a worker from "coding" to "reviewing" to "open the PR," kicking off each step, watching what comes back, and carrying on to the next.
12	
13	The last two feel like a single job, because I do them in the same breath - I drive the work and carry the process in my head at the same time, and never notice they're two different things.
14	
15	Carrying all three myself leaves me with two problems I can't get out from under:
16	
17	1. **Execution is inconsistent.** Because the steps live in my head, the care they get depends on me. On a good day I break the work down and run every step methodically. On a worse one I get lazy, skip a step, or forget part of the process - so the same kind of work gets a different level of care depending on the day.
18	2. **I'm pinned to supervising.** Because the driving eats my attention, I can't do much else while my workers run. I can't step back to think about the next set of projects or to level myself up.
19	
20	So the problem isn't doing the work. It's that holding it all together - the state, the steps, the supervision - falls entirely on me, and the more demanding the work, the worse that gets.
21	
22	## Solution
23	
24	The way out is to stop *being* the thing that holds the work together and build that thing once instead. It rests on three pieces, and they line up with the three things the problem dumps on me - the state, the steps, and the supervision.
25	
26	**One place for state.** Everything starts from a single, organized record of where the work stands: what's done, what's next, what's blocked. Instead of each piece of work's status scattering across loose markdown files, there's one source of truth, and everything else works off it. This is the ground the rest stands on. Of the three, state was the one I already treated as its own job. The other two I carried fused - knowing the work and supervising it, done in the same breath. Pulled apart, each becomes something I can build.
27	
28	**The steps become skills.** Knowing the work is knowing what the steps are, what order they run in, and what standard each one has to meet. That knowledge used to live in my head and come out a little differently every time. I capture it as skills: one skill per step, plus a sequence that runs them in order. The process is written down once, out in the open, the same on every run.
29	
30	**The supervision becomes a supervisor.** The other half is the mechanical part - kick off each step, watch it, check it produced what it should, move to the next, stop and flag anything it can't get past. That's pure mechanics, the part of me that isn't thinking. It gets automated into a supervisor - I call it the drain-cycle - that drives the skills, verifies each result, updates the record, and carries each piece of work along on its own. I'm no longer the connector between steps. I built the connector, and my job moves up a level: I point it at a body of planned work - a cycle now, a whole project later - and let it run.
31	
32	And because the work is planned as a hierarchy - a project holds milestones, a milestone holds tasks - the connector verifies at every altitude, not just the bottom. It checks each task as it lands, then checks each milestone once its tasks are in (did the pieces cohere, did landing them break anything outside their boundary), then checks the project against the goals that can be measured by the end. The connector verifies up the same hierarchy it broke the work down along.
33	
34	**Before - I am the connector**
35	
36	```
37	me     → "write the code for this"
38	agent  → writes it
39	me     → read it. "now review it against these standards"
40	agent  → reviews it
41	me     → "open a PR"
42	agent  → opens it
43	me     → "rewrite the PR description so it reads well"
44	agent  → rewrites it
45	me     → check it, mark it done
46	       → then start the whole chain over for the next piece of work
47	```
48	
49	I'm there at every arrow - for every piece of work in the project. And on a tired day, I skip one.
50	
51	**After - I built the connector**
52	
53	```
54	me         → "work through this project"
55	supervisor → picks up the first piece of work
56	           → runs build → review → PR → finish, checking each result
57	           → marks it done, moves to the next
58	           → ...
59	           → hits one it can't get past review, stops, tells me why
60	me         → review the finished work; step in only where it halted
61	```
62	
63	I'm there once at the start, and again only when something genuinely needs me.
64	
65	**What changes for me**
66	
67	Two things.
68	
69	First, the work gets *more consistent than I am.* Because the steps are captured once and the supervisor runs them the same way every time, execution stops depending on my discipline. It doesn't get tired, skip a step, or forget the process on a bad day, so every piece of work gets the same level of care, not whatever level I happened to have that afternoon.
70	
71	Second, my attention comes off the mechanical loop entirely. Instead of walking each piece of work through the same chain of steps, I move to the work that needs a person: scoping the next project, judging whether finished work is any good, and improving the system itself.
72	
73	And this pays off hardest exactly where the problem hurt most. The more complex the work (the more steps, the more planning) the more the old way leaned on me and the more it broke down. The new way leans on the same captured process and the same supervisor no matter how big the project gets.
74	
```

Verdict and findings below.

---

## Verdict: accept with notes

The SCQA spine is intact and the 30-second grasp holds. No load-bearing span fails a Phase 1-3 rule, so this is not a `reject`. The findings that survive are all Phase-4 line-level (clutter, one nominalization, two metaphors that hide a mechanism, one stress-position miss). None is fatal; all are worth fixing.

---

## Phase 1-3 checks (the spine) — pass

- **Situation** (line 5, sentence 1): "Shipping a piece of agentic work is never one action — it is a sequence..." — a fact the reader accepts immediately. Good.
- **Complication / R1** (line 5, sentences 2-3): hand-driving breaks down as work grows; the operator is "personally holding three fused jobs together." The cost is stated concretely as two named costs. Good — R1 is a visualizable end product (a pinned operator, swinging quality), which satisfies the "concrete cost" requirement even without a number.
- **Question** (line 7): named explicitly and as a real question. Good.
- **Answer / R2** (line 9): "two layers over one shared record," with the payoff stated as an outcome ("execution is consistent on every run... the operator is needed only to point the system... and step in when a run halts"). The answer leads, mechanism follows. Good.
- **No framework vocabulary** leaks (no "Situation"/"Complication"/"SCQA"/"MECE"). Good.
- The three pieces (state / steps / supervision) are introduced in the complication and paid off in the answer's map. The remind-then-expand overlap with §1-§6 is deliberate and reads as intended, not as redundancy.

The 30-second grasp holds: a reader who never sees a diagram gets context (work is a sequence), problem (one person fuses state+steps+supervision, at two costs), and structural answer (two layers, one record, content-blind supervisor) in the first three short paragraphs.

---

## Phase 4 findings (line-level)

**1. Clutter — "personally" is redundant with "by hand."**
Line 5: *"the operator was **personally** holding three fused jobs together"*
The whole sentence is already about hand-driving; "personally" adds nothing the reader doesn't have. Cut it.
Rewrite: *"the operator was holding three fused jobs together"*

**2. Metaphor hides the mechanism — "pinned to driving."**
Line 5: *"the operator stays **pinned to driving** instead of planning the next work"*
Per the style rule "keep the exact term; cut only the hiding metaphor," "pinned to driving" names a feeling, not the mechanism. The mechanism is: the operator has to supervise each transition by hand, so that time can't go to planning.
Rewrite: *"the operator must drive every transition by hand instead of planning the next work."*
(If you want to keep the parallelism with "pinned," weaker alternative: *"the operator is held at the controls instead of planning the next work"* — but the literal verb is clearer.)

**3. Nominalization + weak verb — "and how is that system structured?"**
Line 7: *"...without a person driving it — **and how is that system structured?**"*
Two issues: the question is doubled (the "how do you build X" clause already implies structure), and the tail clause is passive with no actor. The doubling slightly blunts the single-question crispness the lead is going for.
Rewrite (tighten to one question): *"how do you build the thing that holds the work together once — so the workflow runs the same way every time, without a person driving it?"*
If you specifically want to flag that the answer is structural, make the actor the subject: *"...without a person driving it — and how do the pieces fit?"*

**4. Stress-position / old-before-new — the most important term is buried mid-sentence.**
Line 9: *"The move that makes this real is small: `prompt.py` stops inlining the workflow and becomes a thin pointer at `exec:pickup`, so the pack owns the procedure and any vendor's worker follows the same prose."*
The load-bearing new term is `exec:pickup` (the keystone you're previewing), but it sits mid-sentence; the sentence ends on "follows the same prose," which is the consequence, not the new thing. This is fine if "same prose" is the intended payoff, but if the keystone is what you want to land, consider splitting so the mechanism ends on the actor's action.
Rewrite (optional, only if you want the keystone in the stress position): *"The move that makes this real is small: `prompt.py` stops inlining the workflow and becomes a thin pointer at the workflow's front door, `exec:pickup`. The pack now owns the procedure, so any vendor's worker follows the same prose."*

**5. Minor — "closes both costs" is slightly off-idiom.**
Line 9: *"The payoff **closes both costs** above..."*
You close a gap or a cost *gap*; you *eliminate* or *remove* a cost. Small, but "closes both costs" makes the reader re-parse.
Rewrite: *"The payoff removes both costs above..."* or *"The payoff answers both costs above..."* (if you want to keep the closing-the-loop flavor).

---

## Non-findings (deliberately not flagged)

- "remind then expand" overlap with the numbered sections — by design, per your brief; reads as a preview, not repetition.
- Absence of explicit S/C/Q/A headers — correct; the skill forbids them in output.
- No invented numbers — the lead correctly leans on visualizable costs rather than fabricating a metric. Good call given there's no real number to cite here.
- "content-blind supervisor" / "state plane" / "artifact boundary" — precise terms that earn their place; not metaphors-that-hide. Left alone.

---

If you fix items 1-3 and 5 (all unambiguous), the lead is a clean `accept`. Item 4 is a judgment call on whether the keystone or the payoff deserves the stress position — your call.

Both work off one **state plane** — a single authoritative record of where the work stands. The move that makes this real is small: `prompt.py` stops inlining the workflow and becomes a thin pointer at the workflow's front door, `exec:pickup`. The pack then owns the procedure, so any vendor's worker follows the same prose. The payoff removes both costs above — the workflow is captured once and a content-blind supervisor runs it unattended, so execution is consistent on every run instead of varying by the day, and the operator is needed only to point the system at planned work and to step in when a run halts."
}

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/drain-cycle/docs/architecture.md",
  "content": "# Architecture\n\nPre-read: [docs/vision.md](vision.md)\n\nShipping a piece of agentic work is never one action — it is a sequence: code it, review it against the right standards, open the PR, make the description read well. A capable agent handles any one of these steps, one at a time, and driving each by hand holds up fine while the work is small. As the work grows more complex, that hand-driving breaks down — and what breaks reveals that the operator was holding three fused jobs together: the **state** (where the work stands), the **steps** (what runs, in what order, to what standard), and the **supervision** (moving each piece from one step to the next). Holding all three by hand carries two costs: execution quality swings with the operator's discipline on the day, and the operator must drive every transition by hand instead of planning the next work.\n\nSo the question this architecture answers: how do you build the thing that holds the work together once — so the workflow runs the same way every time, without a person driving it — and how do the pieces of that system fit together?\n\nThe answer is two layers over one shared record. **Layer 1 — supervision** (`drain-cycle`) drives the work and grades each result. **Layer 2 — workflow** (the Shaper pack) is the procedure itself, captured as composable skills. They meet at an **artifact boundary**: Layer 2 writes signals, Layer 1 reads them, and neither reaches inside the other. Both work off one **state plane** — a single authoritative record of where the work stands. The move that makes this real is small: `prompt.py` stops inlining the workflow and becomes a thin pointer at the workflow's front door, `exec:pickup`. The pack then owns the procedure, so any vendor's worker follows the same prose. The payoff removes both costs above — the workflow is captured once and a content-blind supervisor runs it unattended, so execution is consistent on every run instead of varying by the day, and the operator is needed only to point the system at planned work and to step in when a run halts.\n\n## 1. Two layers, one product\n\nThe work splits into two layers:\n\n- **Layer 1 — supervision (`drain-cycle`).** Picks up a body of planned work - spawns a worker per phase per issue, holds the guardrails, halts/reverts/resumes/recovers, and records and grades the outcome. It is vendor-agnostic: each worker is a `claude -p` (or any equivalent) subprocess.\n- **Layer 2 — workflow (the Shaper pack).** The intra-issue procedure, captured as composable skills: how a piece of work goes from picked-up to merged PR. This is the knowledge that used to live in the operator's head, written down once.\n\nThe vision's three pieces map onto this split: \"one place for state\" is the state plane (§7), \"the steps become skills\" is Layer 2, and \"the supervision becomes a supervisor\" is Layer 1.\n\n## 2. Layer 2 — the workflow pack\n\nThe pack carries two verb namespaces, one per half of the lifecycle:\n\n- **Planning — `shape:*`.** Four front-door skills: `shape:idea`, `shape:project`, `shape:design`, `shape:delivery`. (Consolidation tracked by the \"Consolidate Shaper's Lifecycle into Four Phase Skills\" project.)\n\n- **Execution — `exec:*`.** The intra-issue graph, named delegations only, no inlined procedure:\n  \n  ```\n  exec:pickup → exec:breakdown → exec:build → (exec:debug | exec:simplify)\n              → exec:review → exec:verify → exec:finish\n  ```\n  \n  `exec:pickup` is the front door; `exec:review` fans out reviewer personas; `exec:finish` is Graphite-first with a plain-git fallback and produces the trail artefacts (What/Why/Focus PR body, review-summary comment, Linear status move). The execution namespace is pinned by ADR 0004 / the Shaper `execution-workflow` design doc; that doc is authoritative for the graph and the handoff contract.\n\nEvery step is also a standalone skill, so a human can run any one by hand. A **handoff envelope** carries the issue's acceptance criteria from pickup through to review, so the spec-compliance persona can grade *built-the-wrong-thing*, not just *built-it-badly*.\n\n## 3. Layer 1 — the supervisor\n\n`drain-cycle` owns process concerns only: spawn, guardrails, halt, revert, resume, recover, record, grade. It reads Linear state and the worker's artifacts to decide whether to advance to the next issue or stop. It does not contain workflow prose — the worker's prompt points at the entry skill and the procedure lives in Layer 2.\n\nThe supervisor mechanics are the bulk of `docs/design-decisions.md`: worktree-per-issue (§3), resource guardrails (§9), group-kill on breach (§8), resume-on-rerun (§14), blocks-aware ordering (§12), and the watch overlay (§15).\n\n## 4. Phase separation: a worker per phase, not a worker per issue\n\nA worker does not run the whole `exec:*` chain. Each phase — code, review, finish — is its own spawn, with its own model tier, and the agent that produced an artifact never judges it. Two independent reasons force this:\n\n- **Independent verification.** A coder reviewing its own work rationalises its own choices. Spawning review as a separate agent that did not write the code makes review adversarial by construction, not by prompt wording.\n- **Per-phase model economics.** Review anchors to a stronger (more expensive) model regardless of what coded the change. That asymmetry is only expressible if each phase is its own spawn with its own `--model` pin — a cheap model can build while an expensive one reviews.\n\nThe three phase agents map onto the existing pack: code = `exec:build` (+`exec:debug`, `exec:simplify`), review = `exec:review`, finish = `exec:finish`. Each is a goal-shaped worker prompt, not a resident process — the goal (\"produce sliced artifacts\", \"apply every quality lens\", \"land human-readable PRs\") drives a sequence of skill delegations inside one spawn.\n\nThe cost accepted in return: every phase boundary pays a spawn plus artifact rehydration, so the handoff envelope (§5) must carry everything the next phase needs — a single worker kept that context in memory for free. This is the deliberate price of independence; see design decision §20.\n\n## 5. The artifact boundary\n\nThe two layers meet at an artifact, not at a function call. **Layer 2 writes signals; Layer 1 reads them.** The supervisor never reads the workflow's steps — only the artifacts the workflow leaves behind: Linear issue state, run-log fields, and `.drain-handoff.json` (e.g. `pr_urls`).\n\nThe placement test for any new behaviour: does `drain-cycle` need to know *what a role does* (Layer 2), or only *whether an artifact exists* (Layer 1)? Submitting a PR is \"what a role does\" — it belongs in a skill. Reading back the submitted PR URLs to decide whether to advance is \"whether an artifact exists\" — it belongs in the supervisor (see design decision §19).\n\nKeeping the boundary at the artifact is what makes Layer 1 content-blind, and content-blindness is what lets the same Layer 2 run under any worker.\n\n## 6. Dual-mode: the same skills by hand or unattended\n\nBecause the workflow is skills and the supervisor only reads artifacts, the same skills run two ways: the operator invokes them at the keyboard, or a spawned worker runs them unattended. There is one workflow, exercised two ways — not a manual path and a separate automated path that drift apart.\n\nDesign decision §10 (config symlinked into each worktree) is what makes the headless worker's environment match an interactive session, so a skill behaves the same in both modes.\n\n## 7. The state plane\n\nEverything works off one organized record of where the work stands. **Linear is authoritative** for issue status (design decision §1, §12). Beneath it sit the durable supervisor records: per-run logs (§4, §8), the atomic active-run marker (§11), and opt-in OpenTelemetry traces (§13).\n\nThese records are the **delayed feedback loop** that replaces live watching. Automating supervision deliberately gives up the operator's real-time view of each worker; observability is how that trade is paid back. Better observability widens the set of work that is safe to hand off (§9).\n\n## 8. The keystone\n\nThe move that realizes the whole split: **`prompt.py` becomes a thin pointer at `exec:pickup`** instead of inlining the workflow. The supervisor stops carrying procedure; the pack owns it; any vendor's worker follows the same prose. This is tracked by the \"drain-cycle supervises; the pack owns the workflow\" project and is the run-first step — everything else in this doc is a facet of it. Until it lands, the supervisor's prompt still names a few skills directly (e.g. the current `/code-review-and-quality` and `/shape:pr-finishing`); those references swap to `exec:*` at the keystone cutover.\n\n## 9. The supervisor's autonomy horizon\n\nHow far the supervisor runs without the operator. Today it drains a cycle transactionally — one issue, one worker, one diff — and halts when it runs out of runnable work or hits a result it cannot get past. The horizon extends outward as observability earns more trust: building on unmerged work, auto-merging trusted classes, and eventually a resident process that watches its own PRs through the review-and-merge loop. That extension is shaped in [`ideas/drain-past-the-merge-gate.md`](ideas/drain-past-the-merge-gate.md).\n\nThe horizon is a deliberate trade, not a default: autonomy is applied selectively where the delayed feedback loop (§7) makes the loss of live intervention acceptable.\n\n## 10. The resident control plane\n\nThe supervisor is moving from a one-shot CLI to a resident process — the autonomy horizon of §9 made concrete. Two scopes, both Layer 1:\n\n- **Control plane (one per machine).** The long-lived daemon. Owns process lifecycle, the queue of planned units to execute, and an API the operator queries and steers: what is running, halt this issue, resume, and (the horizon behaviour) watch open PRs through the review-and-merge loop and respond to review comments.\n- **Execution-coordinator (one per unit in flight).** Spawned by the control plane to drive a single cycle or project. It advances work by reading artifacts (§5) — never by reading inside a phase — and halts on a missing or failed artifact.\n\nThe control plane stays a **process, not a Claude skill** (design decision §22). A `/execute-cycle` skill would run inside a Claude session and collapse the artifact boundary that makes the worker vendor-agnostic; the one-command ergonomics come instead from a thin CLI front-door (`drain-cycle run <unit>`). \"Respond to PR comments\" is a control-plane behaviour, not a pack skill, because it requires watching a PR after it is open — which only a resident process does.\n\n## 11. The execution unit: a cycle or a project\n\nThe supervisor executes a *planned unit*. The atom is unchanged — one issue, with a worker per phase (§4) — and a cycle and a project differ only as containers with a hierarchy over them. \"Drain a cycle\" is one entry point, not the definition; project execution is out of scope today but is a later container, not a redesign (design decision §22). The tool keeps the name `drain-cycle`; the concept it serves is wider.\n\n## 12. Multi-altitude review: the dual of the delivery hierarchy\n\n`shape:delivery` decomposes committed work *downward* — project → milestones → nodes → tasks. Verification rolls *upward* along the same tree, with the review altitude matching the decomposition altitude:\n\n| Altitude             | Fires when                                   | Lenses                                                                                                                             |\n| -------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\n| **Task** (per issue) | a task's diff is ready, before its PR merges | spec-compliance · security · reliability/resilience · code-quality · outcome — diff-bounded                                        |\n| **Milestone**        | a milestone's last child task lands          | integration/coherence/acceptance (the task lens raised a level) · regression (did landing degrade anything outside this boundary?) |\n| **Project**          | a project's last milestone lands             | architecture review · measurable stated goals (partial — some goals take time and are deferred)                                    |\n\nWork fans down; verification rolls up. The trigger is structural — a parent's review fires exactly when its last child completes — and the hierarchy itself is authoritative in Linear (milestones, projects), so the state plane (§7) reads it rather than holding a second copy.\n\nThree consequences distinguish higher-altitude review from task review:\n\n1. **The execution-coordinator is a tree walker, not a queue drainer.** It models the hierarchy, detects \"this milestone's last child landed\", and fires the milestone review.\n2. **Higher-altitude review produces new work, not reverts.** A task review can halt before a PR merges. A milestone or project review runs *after* its child PRs have merged — a merged slice cannot be cleanly reverted — so a failing review emits new remediation issues slotted back into the plan. This is a different halt semantic from task-level, and it widens the open seam below.\n3. **Project review is partial and deferred.** Some goals cannot be measured at completion. Project review measures what it can now and *schedules* the rest, which is only possible with the resident control plane (§10).\n\nTwo unknowns here are not yet decided and are shaped as spikes, not committed: the **blast-radius definition** for regression review (it is not diff-bounded like task review), and **remediation routing** (what gets created on a failing altitude review, and whether the parent pauses or its siblings keep draining). See design decision §21.\n\n## Known open seam\n\nThe one undecided boundary crossing: how Layer-2 verdicts (`outcome_verdict`, `prep_verdict`) travel from a skill into the run-log. Today `handoff.py` carries only `pr_urls`. The verdict-handoff schema is a one-way door for the correctness work (the \"Multi-agent collaboration for correctness\" Layer-1 project) and should be decided before that work starts.\n\nThe multi-altitude reviews (§12) widen this same seam: a milestone or project verdict has to cross the boundary too, and a failing one routes *remediation work* back into the plan rather than recording a pass/fail on a single diff. Whatever schema resolves the task-level verdict handoff should be designed to carry the higher-altitude verdicts as well.\n"
}

336	## 19. The worker owns PR submission via the finishing skill; the orchestrator reads `pr_urls` back
337	
338	This reverses the actor in §16 and §18. There, the orchestrator assembled and submitted the per-repo Graphite stack and posted the Linear comment. Now the **worker** does it: in drain mode the worker commits reviewable slices, then runs the finishing skill (`/shape:pr-finishing` today; `exec:finish` after the keystone cutover, [`architecture.md`](architecture.md) §7), which owns submission — it drives `gt`/`gh`, writes the submitted PR URLs into `.drain-handoff.json` (`pr_urls`), and posts the review-summary comment on the Linear issue. The orchestrator no longer assembles or submits anything; it **reads `pr_urls` back** as confirmation that submission succeeded, and a Done stack-mode issue with no `pr_urls` halts the run rather than letting the next issue stack on an unpushed branch.
339	
340	**Why the reversal.** It is the artifact boundary applied ([`architecture.md`](architecture.md) §2). Submitting a stack is "what a role does" — Layer 2 — so it belongs in a skill that runs identically by hand or unattended. Reading back whether the PRs exist is "whether an artifact exists" — Layer 1 — so it stays in the supervisor. The §16/§18 design put a Layer-2 action inside Layer 1, which is exactly the coupling the two-layer split removes: an orchestrator that knows the `gt`/`gh` sequence cannot be the thin, vendor-agnostic supervisor the keystone (§7) requires.
341	
342	**The verified `gt`/`gh` sequence in §16 is still correct** — it is just run by the finishing skill, not the orchestrator. §16's per-repo preconditions (`gt auth`, `gt init --trunk main`) and its stop-the-line restack policy carry over unchanged.
343	
344	**Completion recovery preserves the boundary.** When a worker exits leaving committed slices but the issue is not properly closed (not Done, or Done-without-`pr_urls`), the orchestrator does not run `gt`/`gh` itself — it spawns a fresh finishing sub-agent that runs the skill, then re-checks the contract, and only halts if completion still fails. A worker that left no committed slices halts as untrusted. (Tracked by the "Orchestrator-enforced completion" delivery plan.)
345	
346	**Alternatives considered.**
347	
348	- *Keep the orchestrator assembling the stack (§16/§18 as written).* Rejected: it hard-codes the PR-tooling sequence into Layer 1, blocking the keystone and the vendor-agnostic worker.
349	- *Worker pushes by hand instead of via the skill.* Rejected: the skill is the single place the submission procedure lives, so it stays identical in interactive and drain modes; a hand-rolled push in the worker prompt would be a second, drifting copy.
350	
351	## 20. A worker per phase, not a worker per issue
352	
353	Today one spawned worker runs the whole `exec:*` chain for an issue — pickup through finish — in a single session. The decision is to split it: each phase (code, review, finish) is its own spawn, with its own model tier, and the agent that produced an artifact never reviews it. See [`architecture.md`](architecture.md) §9.
354	
355	**Why split.** Two independent reasons, either sufficient on its own:
356	
357	- *Independent verification.* A coder reviewing its own work rationalises its own choices — the review inherits the blind spots of the build. A separate review agent that did not write the code is adversarial by construction, not by prompt wording. This is the same logic that made `exec:review` fan out to distinct personas (the persona contract); phase separation extends it across the build/review boundary, not just within review.
358	- *Per-phase model economics.* The operator anchors review to a stronger, more expensive model regardless of what built the change — a cheap model codes, an expensive one reviews. That asymmetry is only expressible if each phase is its own `claude -p` spawn with its own `--model` pin. A single worker pins one model for the whole chain.
359	
360	**Cost accepted.** Every phase boundary now pays a spawn plus artifact rehydration: the next phase starts cold and reads its inputs from the handoff envelope (architecture §2) rather than inheriting them in context. A single worker kept that context for free. This makes the handoff envelope load-bearing for *all* cross-phase state, not just `pr_urls` — the envelope must carry what each phase needs the next to know. This is the deliberate price of independence and the asymmetric-model economics.
361	
362	**Alternatives considered.**
363	
364	- *One worker runs the whole chain (status quo).* Rejected: it forecloses both independent review and per-phase model pinning — the coder grades itself, on the model that built the change.
365	- *One worker, but review re-spawned as a sub-agent within it.* Rejected as a half-measure: it buys independent review but not independent model economics at the phase grain, and it keeps the chain's lifecycle coupled to one outer session that the control plane cannot steer phase-by-phase (§10).
366	
367	## 21. Review is multi-altitude; higher-altitude review yields new work, not reverts
368	
369	Review fires at three altitudes matching the delivery hierarchy `shape:delivery` already produces — task, milestone, project — not only per task. The full model is in [`architecture.md`](architecture.md) §11.
370	
371	**Why multi-altitude.** A task review is diff-bounded: it grades one issue's change against its AC and the quality lenses. It cannot see whether the tasks of a milestone *cohere*, whether landing them degraded something *outside* their own boundary, or whether the project met its stated goals. Those are real defect classes that only exist at a higher altitude, so they need a review oriented to that altitude. Verification rolls up the same tree the work was decomposed down — the dual of the delivery hierarchy.
```
